People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I mean, I try to tell myself it’s just normal stress, but it does kind of jump around. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Um… probably longer than I want to admit. I think it’s been most days for about a year and three months now. It really started after I began the evening graduate certificate program while still keeping my full-time analyst job. I kept thinking it was just a busy season, but… it hasn’t really let up. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around all the time or anything… but I do have this kind of keyed-up feeling a lot, like I should be doing something or I’m forgetting something.\n\nMaybe that counts as on edge? I don’t know—I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind feels preoccupied with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get things done eventually, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, but it definitely happens.\n\nI’ll be staring at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and didn’t actually take any of it in. My mind is already off going through everything that could go wrong. So… yes, I think worry does make it hard to concentrate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try to push through it, so I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or something, but by midafternoon at the office I feel really drained.\n\nSometimes even answering emails feels like it takes a lot of effort, or just getting myself to walk to the subway feels harder than it should. Like one minute I’m worried I’m falling behind at work, then I’m thinking maybe I’m wasting money on school, and then I’m worried my family’s going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but even small issues can start to feel like they could mess up my whole future. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Um… probably longer than I want to admit. I think it’s been most days for about a year and three months now. It really started after I began the evening graduate certificate program while still keeping my full-time analyst job. I kept thinking it was just a busy season, but… it hasn’t really let up. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around all the time or anything… but I do have this kind of keyed-up feeling a lot, like I should be doing something or I’m forgetting something.\n\nMaybe that counts as on edge? I don’t know—I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind feels preoccupied with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get things done eventually, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, but it definitely happens.\n\nI’ll be staring at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and didn’t actually take any of it in. My mind is already off going through everything that could go wrong. So… yes, I think worry does make it hard to concentrate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try to push through it, so I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or something, but by midafternoon at the office I feel really drained.\n\nSometimes even answering emails feels like it takes a lot of effort, or just getting myself to walk to the subway feels harder than it should. And I keep thinking if I just rest on the weekend I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really seem to give me my energy back. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. I mean, sometimes I’m probably a little tight just from sitting at a desk, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if it’s related to anything.\n\nIs that something I should be noticing more?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly, I guess. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed if that’s not the right word.\n\nI’ve had days where I feel kind of low or worn down, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as being depressed. I’m still getting up and doing what I need to do, so… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that. What do you mean by depressed?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d say most days. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of down or discouraged, but I don’t want to overstate it if it’s not really sadness.\n\nMaybe some days I feel low, but I’m still functioning, so I don’t know if that counts. Do you mean like crying or feeling hopeless, or just generally low?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do your worries feel mostly tied to the graduate program and balancing it with work?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, the graduate program and work are a big part of it. But I guess it kind of spreads out from there.\n\nLike, my mind jumps from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to wondering if school is a waste of money, to thinking my family is going to decide I can’t handle things. And then some small thing happens, and I react like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I’m trying not to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring the past two weeks, have you felt hopeless?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / management_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds kind of strong, and I don’t want to overstate it.

I do have moments where I feel really discouraged, like I can’t quite see how I’m supposed to keep up with everything, but I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling hopeless. Maybe more overwhelmed than hopeless? Does that distinction make sense?

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lying there panicking every single night, but usually it’s my mind not stopping.

Even after I close my laptop and get into bed, I’ll keep mentally going back over things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m checking for something I forgot. And then hours can go by.

I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Um… probably longer than I want to admit. I think it’s been most days for about a year and three months now. It really started after I began the evening graduate certificate program while still keeping my full-time analyst job. I kept thinking it was just a busy season, but… it hasn’t really let up. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around all the time or anything… but I do have this kind of keyed-up feeling a lot, like I should be doing something or I’m forgetting something.\n\nMaybe that counts as on edge? I don’t know—I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind feels preoccupied with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get things done eventually, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, but it definitely happens.\n\nI’ll be staring at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and didn’t actually take any of it in. My mind is already off going through everything that could go wrong. So… yes, I think worry does make it hard to concentrate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try to push through it, so I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or something, but by midafternoon at the office I feel really drained.\n\nSometimes even answering emails feels like it takes a lot of effort, or just getting myself to walk to the subway feels harder than it should. And I keep thinking if I just rest on the weekend I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really seem to give me my energy back. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. I mean, sometimes I’m probably a little tight just from sitting at a desk, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if it’s related to anything.\n\nIs that something I should be noticing more?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly, I guess. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed if that’s not the right word.\n\nI’ve had days where I feel kind of low or worn down, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as being depressed. I’m still getting up and doing what I need to do, so… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that. What do you mean by depressed?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d say most days. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of down or discouraged, but I don’t want to overstate it if it’s not really sadness.\n\nMaybe some days I feel low, but I’m still functioning, so I don’t know if that counts. Do you mean like crying or feeling hopeless, or just generally low?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. I still do some of the things I usually like, but maybe I don’t always feel as excited about them as I think I should.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as losing pleasure, though. Like, is it more about not wanting to do anything at all, or just not enjoying things as much?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying as much, are you able to fall asleep without much trouble?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I wish I could say yes, but even on nights where I don’t feel like I’m actively worrying as much, I still end up lying there for a long time.\n\nI’ll have my laptop closed and everything, but my brain keeps kind of rechecking things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m still mentally trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. And even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do your worries feel mostly tied to the graduate program and balancing it with work?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, the graduate program and work are a big part of it. But I guess it kind of spreads out from there.\n\nLike, my mind jumps from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to wondering if school is a waste of money, to thinking my family is going to decide I can’t handle things. And then some small thing happens, and I react like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I’m trying not to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: During the past two weeks, have you felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds kind of strong, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do have moments where I feel really discouraged, like I can’t quite see how I’m supposed to keep up with everything, but I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling hopeless. Maybe more overwhelmed than hopeless? Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, does it usually feel like worrying is what’s keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lying there panicking every single night, but usually it’s my mind not stopping.\n\nEven after I close my laptop and get into bed, I’ll keep mentally going back over things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m checking for something I forgot. And then hours can go by.\n\nSo yes, I guess worrying is a big part of what keeps me awake. Even when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really turned off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually one of the harder parts.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, like, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” but then that one worry turns into another one almost immediately. So I’ll start checking my calendar, then my budget, then my assignments, and then I’ll go back and check them again instead of actually doing anything useful.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive, but it’s like once it starts, I have a really hard time making it stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates":["F41.1","F51.0","F43.22","F32.A"],"note":"","is_final":false}

I kept thinking it was just a busy season, but… it hasn’t really let up. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around all the time or anything… but I do have this kind of keyed-up feeling a lot, like I should be doing something or I’m forgetting something.\n\nMaybe that counts as on edge? I don’t know—I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind feels preoccupied with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get things done eventually, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, but it definitely happens.\n\nI’ll be staring at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and didn’t actually take any of it in. My mind is already off going through everything that could go wrong. So… yes, I think worry does make it hard to concentrate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try to push through it, so I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or something, but by midafternoon at the office I feel really drained.\n\nSometimes even answering emails feels like it takes a lot of effort, or just getting myself to walk to the subway feels harder than it should. And I keep thinking if I just rest on the weekend I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really seem to give me my energy back. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. I mean, sometimes I’m probably a little tight just from sitting at a desk, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if it’s related to anything.\n\nIs that something I should be noticing more?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly, I guess. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed if that’s not the right word.\n\nI’ve had days where I feel kind of low or worn down, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as being depressed. I’m still getting up and doing what I need to do, so… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that. What do you mean by depressed?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d say most days. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of down or discouraged, but I don’t want to overstate it if it’s not really sadness.\n\nMaybe some days I feel low, but I’m still functioning, so I don’t know if that counts. Do you mean like crying or feeling hopeless, or just generally low?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. I still do some of the things I usually like, but maybe I don’t always feel as excited about them as I think I should.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as losing pleasure, though. Like, is it more about not wanting to do anything at all, or just not enjoying things as much?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying as much, are you able to fall asleep without much trouble?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I wish I could say yes, but even on nights where I don’t feel like I’m actively worrying as much, I still end up lying there for a long time.\n\nI’ll have my laptop closed and everything, but my brain keeps kind of rechecking things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m still mentally trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. And even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do your worries feel mostly tied to the graduate program and balancing it with work?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, the graduate program and work are a big part of it. But I guess it kind of spreads out from there.\n\nLike, my mind jumps from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to wondering if school is a waste of money, to thinking my family is going to decide I can’t handle things. And then some small thing happens, and I react like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I’m trying not to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: During the past two weeks, have you felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds kind of strong, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do have moments where I feel really discouraged, like I can’t quite see how I’m supposed to keep up with everything, but I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling hopeless. Maybe more overwhelmed than hopeless? Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, does it usually feel like worrying is what’s keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lying there panicking every single night, but usually it’s my mind not stopping.\n\nEven after I close my laptop and get into bed, I’ll keep mentally going back over things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m checking for something I forgot. And then hours can go by.\n\nSo yes, I guess worrying is a big part of what keeps me awake. Even when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really turned off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually one of the harder parts.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, like, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” but then that one worry turns into another one almost immediately. So I’ll start checking my calendar, then my budget, then my assignments, and then I’ll go back and check them again instead of actually doing anything useful.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive, but it’s like once it starts, I have a really hard time making it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I feel a little embarrassed saying that, because sometimes the things I’m worried about are real things, but my reaction feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nLike my mind will jump from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “Am I wasting money on school?” to “What if my family thinks I can’t handle my life?” And then even something small starts to feel like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that may sound dramatic, and I don’t mean to be, but… yes, I think the worrying is more than the situation calls for a lot of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I think it’s been most days for about a year and three months now. It really started after I began the evening graduate certificate program while still keeping my full-time analyst job. I kept thinking it was just a busy season, but… it hasn’t really let up. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around all the time or anything… but I do have this kind of keyed-up feeling a lot, like I should be doing something or I’m forgetting something.\n\nMaybe that counts as on edge? I don’t know—I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind feels preoccupied with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get things done eventually, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, but it definitely happens.\n\nI’ll be staring at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and didn’t actually take any of it in. My mind is already off going through everything that could go wrong. So… yes, I think worry does make it hard to concentrate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try to push through it, so I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or something, but by midafternoon at the office I feel really drained.\n\nSometimes even answering emails feels like it takes a lot of effort, or just getting myself to walk to the subway feels harder than it should. And I keep thinking if I just rest on the weekend I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really seem to give me my energy back. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. I mean, sometimes I’m probably a little tight just from sitting at a desk, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if it’s related to anything.\n\nIs that something I should be noticing more?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly, I guess. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed if that’s not the right word.\n\nI’ve had days where I feel kind of low or worn down, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as being depressed. I’m still getting up and doing what I need to do, so… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that. What do you mean by depressed?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d say most days. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of down or discouraged, but I don’t want to overstate it if it’s not really sadness.\n\nMaybe some days I feel low, but I’m still functioning, so I don’t know if that counts. Do you mean like crying or feeling hopeless, or just generally low?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. I still do some of the things I usually like, but maybe I don’t always feel as excited about them as I think I should.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as losing pleasure, though. Like, is it more about not wanting to do anything at all, or just not enjoying things as much?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying as much, are you able to fall asleep without much trouble?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I wish I could say yes, but even on nights where I don’t feel like I’m actively worrying as much, I still end up lying there for a long time.\n\nI’ll have my laptop closed and everything, but my brain keeps kind of rechecking things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m still mentally trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. And even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do your worries feel mostly tied to the graduate program and balancing it with work?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, the graduate program and work are a big part of it. But I guess it kind of spreads out from there.\n\nLike, my mind jumps from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to wondering if school is a waste of money, to thinking my family is going to decide I can’t handle things. And then some small thing happens, and I react like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I’m trying not to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: During the past two weeks, have you felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds kind of strong, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do have moments where I feel really discouraged, like I can’t quite see how I’m supposed to keep up with everything, but I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling hopeless. Maybe more overwhelmed than hopeless? Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, does it usually feel like worrying is what’s keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lying there panicking every single night, but usually it’s my mind not stopping.\n\nEven after I close my laptop and get into bed, I’ll keep mentally going back over things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m checking for something I forgot. And then hours can go by.\n\nSo yes, I guess worrying is a big part of what keeps me awake. Even when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really turned off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually one of the harder parts.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, like, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” but then that one worry turns into another one almost immediately. So I’ll start checking my calendar, then my budget, then my assignments, and then I’ll go back and check them again instead of actually doing anything useful.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive, but it’s like once it starts, I have a really hard time making it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I feel a little embarrassed saying that, because sometimes the things I’m worried about are real things, but my reaction feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nLike my mind will jump from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “Am I wasting money on school?” to “What if my family thinks I can’t handle my life?” And then even something small starts to feel like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that may sound dramatic, and I don’t mean to be, but… yes, I think the worrying is more than the situation calls for a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe a little? I don’t think of myself as an angry person, and I really try not to take things out on people.\n\nSometimes I notice I’m more short than I want to be, like small things bother me more than they should. But I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually” irritable. Maybe you can tell me what you mean by that?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Like one minute I’m worried I’m falling behind at work, then I’m thinking maybe I’m wasting money on school, and then I’m worried my family’s going to think I can’t handle things.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but even small issues can start to feel like they could mess up my whole future. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been worrying like this on most days?\n\nPatient: Um… probably longer than I want to admit. I think it’s been most days for about a year and three months now. It really started after I began the evening graduate certificate program while still keeping my full-time analyst job. I kept thinking it was just a busy season, but… it hasn’t really let up. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to judge that. I wouldn’t say I’m, like, pacing around all the time or anything… but I do have this kind of keyed-up feeling a lot, like I should be doing something or I’m forgetting something.\n\nMaybe that counts as on edge? I don’t know—I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind feels preoccupied with worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get things done eventually, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, but it definitely happens.\n\nI’ll be staring at the same financial model at work, or a reading for class, and realize I’ve gone over the same paragraph like five times and didn’t actually take any of it in. My mind is already off going through everything that could go wrong. So… yes, I think worry does make it hard to concentrate.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try to push through it, so I don’t want to sound like I’m just being lazy or something, but by midafternoon at the office I feel really drained.\n\nSometimes even answering emails feels like it takes a lot of effort, or just getting myself to walk to the subway feels harder than it should. And I keep thinking if I just rest on the weekend I’ll bounce back, but it doesn’t really seem to give me my energy back. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really paid attention to that specifically. I mean, sometimes I’m probably a little tight just from sitting at a desk, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or if it’s related to anything.\n\nIs that something I should be noticing more?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly, I guess. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed if that’s not the right word.\n\nI’ve had days where I feel kind of low or worn down, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as being depressed. I’m still getting up and doing what I need to do, so… I guess I’m not totally sure how to answer that. What do you mean by depressed?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d say most days. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of down or discouraged, but I don’t want to overstate it if it’s not really sadness.\n\nMaybe some days I feel low, but I’m still functioning, so I don’t know if that counts. Do you mean like crying or feeling hopeless, or just generally low?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve completely lost interest, exactly. I still do some of the things I usually like, but maybe I don’t always feel as excited about them as I think I should.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as losing pleasure, though. Like, is it more about not wanting to do anything at all, or just not enjoying things as much?\n\nDoctor: On nights when you’re not worrying as much, are you able to fall asleep without much trouble?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really. I mean, I wish I could say yes, but even on nights where I don’t feel like I’m actively worrying as much, I still end up lying there for a long time.\n\nI’ll have my laptop closed and everything, but my brain keeps kind of rechecking things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m still mentally trying to make sure I didn’t miss something. And even when I do fall asleep, I wake up feeling like I never really shut off. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do your worries feel mostly tied to the graduate program and balancing it with work?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, the graduate program and work are a big part of it. But I guess it kind of spreads out from there.\n\nLike, my mind jumps from worrying I’m falling behind at work, to wondering if school is a waste of money, to thinking my family is going to decide I can’t handle things. And then some small thing happens, and I react like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that probably sounds dramatic. I’m trying not to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s kind of how it feels in my head.\n\nDoctor: During the past two weeks, have you felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That sounds kind of strong, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nI do have moments where I feel really discouraged, like I can’t quite see how I’m supposed to keep up with everything, but I don’t know if that’s the same as feeling hopeless. Maybe more overwhelmed than hopeless? Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you can’t fall asleep, does it usually feel like worrying is what’s keeping you awake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m lying there panicking every single night, but usually it’s my mind not stopping.\n\nEven after I close my laptop and get into bed, I’ll keep mentally going back over things—work deadlines, tuition payments, class assignments—like I’m checking for something I forgot. And then hours can go by.\n\nSo yes, I guess worrying is a big part of what keeps me awake. Even when I finally do sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really turned off, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually one of the harder parts.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, like, “Okay, stop, just focus on this one thing,” but then that one worry turns into another one almost immediately. So I’ll start checking my calendar, then my budget, then my assignments, and then I’ll go back and check them again instead of actually doing anything useful.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little excessive, but it’s like once it starts, I have a really hard time making it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I feel a little embarrassed saying that, because sometimes the things I’m worried about are real things, but my reaction feels bigger than what’s actually happening.\n\nLike my mind will jump from, “Am I falling behind at work?” to “Am I wasting money on school?” to “What if my family thinks I can’t handle my life?” And then even something small starts to feel like it could mess up my whole future.\n\nI know that may sound dramatic, and I don’t mean to be, but… yes, I think the worrying is more than the situation calls for a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… maybe a little? I don’t think of myself as an angry person, and I really try not to take things out on people.\n\nSometimes I notice I’m more short than I want to be, like small things bother me more than they should. But I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually” irritable. Maybe you can tell me what you mean by that?\n\nDoctor: Did your anxiety start after you began the graduate program while working full-time?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s about right. It started after I began the evening graduate certificate program while still working full-time as an analyst.\n\nI guess it’s been around a year and three months now. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when it really became noticeable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
